Lastly, we need to activate \texttt{tidyverse}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{suppressMessages}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{require}\NormalTok{(tidyverse))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Tests for Quantitative
Variables}\label{tests-for-quantitative-variables}

\subsection{One-sample t-tests}\label{sec-04-one-sample-t-test}

The goal of a one-sample t-test is to test if the mean of a quantitative
variable is equal to a certain value. For example, if the aviation
regulatory agency states that acceptable flight delay is 12 minutes. We
want to see if the flights meet the requirement. The null hypothesis
(\(H_{0}\)) is that the mean delay is equal to 12 minutes. The
alternative hypothesis (\(H_{A}\)) is that the mean delay is not equal
to 12 minutes. Here are the steps to perform such a test:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{t.test}\NormalTok{(FlightDelays}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Delay, }\AttributeTok{mu =} \DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}

    One Sample t-test

data:  FlightDelays$Delay
t = -0.39963, df = 4028, p-value = 0.6895
alternative hypothesis: true mean is not equal to 12
95 percent confidence interval:
 10.45205 13.02375
sample estimates:
mean of x 
  11.7379 
\end{verbatim}

As the p-value (0.6895) is greater than \(\alpha\) = 0.05 at a 95\%
confidence level, we fail to reject the null hypothesis \(H_{0}\),
meaning that the mean flight delay meets the standard.

\subsection{One-sided t-tests}\label{one-sided-t-tests}

In the above flight delay example, the condition to reject the \(H_{0}\)
is that the delay time is either much longer or much shorter than 12
minutes (that is why the test is called two-sided). Indisputably, the
latter case does not make much sense, as short delay times indicate the
flights are punctual. Hence, the test must focus on long delay times by
replacing the above two-sided test with a one-sided test in which the
rejection region (\(H_{A}\)) lies at larger t-statistics (on the right).
The parameter \texttt{alternative="greater"} suggests a one-sided test
where the rejection region lies at the greater value.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{t.test}\NormalTok{(FlightDelays}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Delay, }\AttributeTok{mu =} \DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{alternative =} \StringTok{"greater"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}

    One Sample t-test

data:  FlightDelays$Delay
t = -0.39963, df = 4028, p-value = 0.6553
alternative hypothesis: true mean is greater than 12
95 percent confidence interval:
 10.65885      Inf
sample estimates:
mean of x 
  11.7379 
\end{verbatim}

The p-value 0.6553 is larger than \(\alpha\) = 0.05, fail to reject the
\(H_{0}\) at a 95\% significance level.

\subsection{Two-sample t-tests}\label{sec-04-two-sample-t-test}

A two-sample t-test tests whether the mean of one group is the same as
the other group. The \(H_{0}\) states the two groups have the same mean,
and the \(H_{A}\) states otherwise.

For example, we want to compare the mean delays of the two carriers to
determine whether they are the same.

\emph{Solution 1:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{AA\_delay }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(FlightDelays, Carrier}\SpecialCharTok{==}\StringTok{"AA"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{UA\_delay }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(FlightDelays, Carrier}\SpecialCharTok{==}\StringTok{"UA"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{t.test}\NormalTok{(AA\_delay}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Delay, UA\_delay}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Delay)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}

    Welch Two Sample t-test

data:  AA_delay$Delay and UA_delay$Delay
t = -3.8255, df = 1843.8, p-value = 0.0001349
alternative hypothesis: true difference in means is not equal to 0
95 percent confidence interval:
 -8.903198 -2.868194
sample estimates:
mean of x mean of y 
 10.09738  15.98308 
\end{verbatim}

Based on the p-value (0.0001349) being less than \(\alpha\) = 0.05, the
delays of the two carriers are different. The bottom of the test results
shows that the mean delay for AA (group x) is 10.09738, shorter than
UA's 15.98308.

\emph{Solution 2:}

This approach makes use of the categorical variable \texttt{carrier} to
split the data set into two, avoiding the creation of two temporary data
frames.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{t.test}\NormalTok{(Delay }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ Carrier, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{FlightDelays)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}

    Welch Two Sample t-test

data:  Delay by Carrier
t = -3.8255, df = 1843.8, p-value = 0.0001349
alternative hypothesis: true difference in means between group AA and group UA is not equal to 0
95 percent confidence interval:
 -8.903198 -2.868194
sample estimates:
mean in group AA mean in group UA 
        10.09738         15.98308 
\end{verbatim}

The symbol \texttt{\textasciitilde{}} means ``split by''.
\texttt{Delay\ \textasciitilde{}\ Carrier} means to split \texttt{Delay}
by \texttt{Carrier}.

\subsection{One-way ANOVA}\label{sec-04-one-way-ANOVA}

A one-way ANOVA tests whether the means of three or more groups are the
same. Two key differences from the two-sample t-test above: 1. a
two-sample t-test can handle only two groups, but ANOVA can handle two
or more groups. 2. The \(H_{0}\) of ANOVA is rejected if any pair(s) of
groups have unequal means.

For example, test whether mean delays vary by destination.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{aov\_results }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{aov}\NormalTok{(Delay }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ Destination, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{FlightDelays)}
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(aov\_results)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
              Df  Sum Sq Mean Sq F value   Pr(>F)    
Destination    6   62891   10482   6.094 2.29e-06 ***
Residuals   4022 6918028    1720                     
---
Signif. codes:  0 '***' 0.001 '**' 0.01 '*' 0.05 '.' 0.1 ' ' 1
\end{verbatim}

If you only care whether the \(H_{0}\) can be rejected, focus on the
overall p-value (2.29e-06) under the \texttt{Pr(\textgreater{}F)} column
on the right. It is undeniably smaller than \(\alpha\) = 0.05, so the
\(H_{0}\) can be rejected.

In some situations, you may be interested in finding out which pair(s)
do not have the same mean. Tukey's Honest Significant Difference (HSD)
test can answer this question.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{TukeyHSD}\NormalTok{(aov\_results)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
  Tukey multiple comparisons of means
    95% family-wise confidence level

Fit: aov(formula = Delay ~ Destination, data = FlightDelays)

$Destination
               diff         lwr        upr     p adj
DEN-BNA   7.1454369  -4.8424432 19.1333171 0.5765602
DFW-BNA   3.2646932  -6.8999706 13.4293570 0.9647119
IAD-BNA  19.4461945   0.4951700 38.3972191 0.0399134
MIA-BNA  12.9137057   2.3518660 23.4755453 0.0058064
ORD-BNA   8.4338186  -1.3335463 18.2011835 0.1430105
STL-BNA   0.9181137 -11.4728506 13.3090780 0.9999910
DFW-DEN  -3.8807437 -12.4245377  4.6630503 0.8331281
IAD-DEN  12.3007576  -5.8325642 30.4340793 0.4140643
MIA-DEN   5.7682688  -3.2444158 14.7809533 0.4884472
ORD-DEN   1.2883817  -6.7786774  9.3554408 0.9991892
STL-DEN  -6.2273232 -17.3274143  4.8727678 0.6463375
IAD-DFW  16.1815013  -0.8016809 33.1646835 0.0736782
MIA-DFW   9.6490125   3.2584254 16.0395995 0.0001755
ORD-DFW   5.1691254   0.2003667 10.1378841 0.0352035
STL-DFW  -2.3465795 -11.4473015  6.7541425 0.9885164
MIA-IAD  -6.5324888 -23.7563253 10.6913477 0.9225580
ORD-IAD -11.0123759 -27.7607938  5.7360421 0.4540417
STL-IAD -18.5280808 -36.9303655 -0.1257961 0.0471723
ORD-MIA  -4.4798870 -10.2175372  1.2577631 0.2425378
STL-MIA -11.9955920 -21.5378772 -2.4533067 0.0039784
STL-ORD  -7.5157049 -16.1704244  1.1390145 0.1382159
\end{verbatim}

It lists all possible pairs of destinations. Since there are seven
destinations, the total number of pairs is 21 (\texttt{choose(7,2)}).

Each row shows the difference of means (\texttt{diff}), the lower
(\texttt{lwr}) and upper (\texttt{upr}) bounds of the confidence
interval, and the adjusted p-value (\texttt{p\ adj}). Examining the
p-value is the quickest way to identify pairs with unequal means. E.g.,
IAD-BNA in which the p-value is less than 0.05.

To ease analysis, it is suggested to plot the HSD result as below:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{hsd\_results }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{TukeyHSD}\NormalTok{(aov\_results)}

\CommentTok{\# las=1 causes the plot to print the pair name horizontally.}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(hsd\_results, }\AttributeTok{las=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{04_Statistical_Tests_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-12-1.pdf}}

Confidence intervals not crossing zero (the vertical line) indicate
pairs have unequal means.

\subsection{Paired t-tests}\label{sec-04-paired-t-test}

A paired t-test evaluates whether the mean difference between two
measurements taken on the same subject significantly differs from zero.
For a drug trial example, a subject's blood pressure is measured before
and after taking the tested drug. Each sample is a subject. The
measurements are blood pressure. The \(H_{0}\) is that the mean
difference between the two measurements is zero.

Here we will examine whether the product prices between Target and
Walmart are different. The subject is a product. The two measurements
are the prices of the same product from Target and Walmart.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{with}\NormalTok{(Groceries, }\FunctionTok{t.test}\NormalTok{(Target, Walmart, }\AttributeTok{paired=}\NormalTok{T))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}

    Paired t-test

data:  Target and Walmart
t = 0.47046, df = 29, p-value = 0.6415
alternative hypothesis: true mean difference is not equal to 0
95 percent confidence interval:
 -0.1896825  0.3030159
sample estimates:
mean difference 
     0.05666667 
\end{verbatim}

The p-value of 0.6415 \textgreater{} \(\alpha\) = 0.05 suggests that
there is no statistical difference between the two stores at the 95\%
confidence level. The mean price difference of all the products in the
sample is 0.057, which is not considered a significant difference from
zero.

\section{Tests for Categorical
Variables}\label{tests-for-categorical-variables}

\subsection{One Categorical Variable}\label{sec-04-x2-gof-test}

Here, we want to check whether the proportions of categories match a
specified expected ratio. In standard textbooks, this test is called the
\textbf{Chi-square (}\(\chi^{2}\)\textbf{) goodness-of-fit} test. For
example, \texttt{Delayed30} indicates whether the flight was delayed by
more than 30 minutes; it is either Yes or No.~Suppose the regulatory
standard recommends not more than 10\% of a random sample of flights.
First, figure out how many Yes and No in the sample.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{table}\NormalTok{(FlightDelays}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Delayed30)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}

  No  Yes 
3432  597 
\end{verbatim}

The data set contains 4,029 flights; 3,432 were punctual, and 587 were
delayed. According to the standard, the number of delayed flights should
be no more than 10\% of the total, or 402. Due to random variation in
sampling, we cannot conclude an issue is present simply because 587 is
greater than 402. Here's where the \(\chi^2\)-test comes in.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{chisq.test}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{table}\NormalTok{(FlightDelays}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Delayed30), }\AttributeTok{p=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.9}\NormalTok{,}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}

    Chi-squared test for given probabilities

data:  table(FlightDelays$Delayed30)
X-squared = 103.9, df = 1, p-value < 2.2e-16
\end{verbatim}

The \(H_{0}\) assumes the proportions agree with the expected ratio. The
p-value shown above is literally zero, indicating that the proportions
of Yes and No do not conform to the expected 9:1 ratio at the 95\%
confidence level.

One remark about setting up the proportion parameter
\texttt{p=c(0.9,0.1)}. Make sure that the order of the proportions
matches the sequence of the categories produced by the \texttt{table()}
function. Additionally, the proportions must sum to 1.

\subsection{Two Categorical
Variables}\label{sec-04-x2-test-independence}

Here we examine the independence between two categorical variables. The
test is colloquially called \(\chi^{2}\) \textbf{Test for Independence}.
For example, we want to know whether a delay of more than 30 minutes
depends on the carrier, that is to say whether \texttt{Delayed30} is
independent of \texttt{Carrier}. The \(H_{0}\) assumes that they are
independent.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{with}\NormalTok{(FlightDelays, }\FunctionTok{chisq.test}\NormalTok{(Carrier, Delayed30, }\AttributeTok{correct=}\ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}

    Pearson's Chi-squared test with Yates' continuity correction

data:  Carrier and Delayed30
X-squared = 13.462, df = 1, p-value = 0.0002434
\end{verbatim}

Note that \texttt{correct=TRUE} instructs the test function to make
adjustments in calculating the \(\chi^2\) statistic. The correction is
only applicable for a 2x2 contingency table with small counts. However,
it does little harm if you turn it on for tables with a different
dimension or large counts\footnote{\url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yates\%27s_correction_for_continuity}}.
My suggestion is to turn it on all the time.

As the p-value of 0.0002434 is less than 0.05, reject the \(H_{0}\) at
the 95\% confidence level, meaning that \texttt{Delay30} and
\texttt{Carrier} are not independent. This conclusion suggests that the
proportion of delayed flights differs between the two carriers. To be
clear, their dependency says nothing about the carriers' delay issue. To
figure that out exactly, we should examine the proportion of delayed
flights for each carrier using a new function \texttt{prop.table()} as
below:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{with}\NormalTok{(FlightDelays, }\FunctionTok{prop.table}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{table}\NormalTok{(Carrier, Delayed30), }\AttributeTok{margin=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
       Delayed30
Carrier        No       Yes
     AA 0.8647626 0.1352374
     UA 0.8183437 0.1816563
\end{verbatim}

We feed the contingency table of \texttt{Carrier} by \texttt{Delayed30}
to the \texttt{prop.table()} function with \texttt{margin=1}, meaning
that values are divided by the row totals or the sum of the proportions
for each row is 1. As you can see, both carriers have an issue under the
10\% rule, but the issue with UA is more severe than AA.

\section{Regression}\label{regression}

\subsection{Simple Linear Regression}\label{sec-04-SLR}

A simple linear regression (SLR) model involves a predictor X
(independent variable) and a response Y (dependent variable), where both
of them are quantitative, but they have different roles.

\[Y = \beta_{0} + \beta_{1}X\]

where \(\beta_{0}\) is the intercept, and \(\beta_{1}\) is the slope.

The relationship between them in an SLR is linear and directional.
Firstly, the predictor is believed to influence the response, and not
the reverse; therefore, it is directional. For example, temperature
(predictor) is supposed to influence ice cream sales (response), and not
the other way.

Secondly, the relationship is linear, meaning that a unit increment of X
will cause Y to increase or decrease by \(\beta_{1}\) units for every X
within the range of X in the data.

Thirdly, an SLR can only establish a correlation between X and Y, but
not causation.

Finally, in the context of NHST, the \(H_{0}\) is \(\beta_{1}\) = 0.

Here we will switch to the familiar built-in \texttt{iris} data set to
demonstrate how to build an SLR. Suppose we are interested in whether
\texttt{Sep.Length} (predictor) affects \texttt{Sep.Width} (response)
for \texttt{setosa} species. The first step for building an SLR is to
visualize the relationship between the two variables using a scatter
plot (Section~\ref{sec-baseR-scatter-plot} or
Section~\ref{sec-ggplot-scatter-plot}). Do not downplay the intuition of
visual inspection. In the absence of an upward or downward trend, the
attempt to build an SLR is deemed fruitless. On a positive note, the
plot may still reveal an intriguing trend not as expected, requiring a
different kind of model or variable transformation. Anyway, let's plot
the two variables.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{setosa\_iris }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(iris, Species}\SpecialCharTok{==}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}setosa\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{setosa\_iris, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x=}\NormalTok{Sepal.Length, }\AttributeTok{y=}\NormalTok{Sepal.Width)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{04_Statistical_Tests_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-18-1.pdf}}

As shown above, there is an upward trend even though it is not
definitive for everyone. Subjectively, we will proceed to build an SLR
model.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{slr\_model }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{lm}\NormalTok{(Sepal.Width }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ Sepal.Length, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{setosa\_iris)}
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(slr\_model)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}

Call:
lm(formula = Sepal.Width ~ Sepal.Length, data = setosa_iris)

Residuals:
     Min       1Q   Median       3Q      Max 
-0.72394 -0.18273 -0.00306  0.15738  0.51709 

Coefficients:
             Estimate Std. Error t value Pr(>|t|)    
(Intercept)   -0.5694     0.5217  -1.091    0.281    
Sepal.Length   0.7985     0.1040   7.681 6.71e-10 ***
---
Signif. codes:  0 '***' 0.001 '**' 0.01 '*' 0.05 '.' 0.1 ' ' 1

Residual standard error: 0.2565 on 48 degrees of freedom
Multiple R-squared:  0.5514,    Adjusted R-squared:  0.542 
F-statistic: 58.99 on 1 and 48 DF,  p-value: 6.71e-10
\end{verbatim}

The slope and the intercept can be found under the \texttt{Estimate}
column of \texttt{Coefficients} subsection. In this example, the
intercept \(\beta_{0}\) is -0.5694, and the slope \(\beta_{1}\)
(\texttt{Sepal.Length}) is 0.7985. So the SLR model is:

\[Sepal.Width = -0.5694 + 0.7985 \times Sepal.Length   \]

The SLR model says that a unit increase in \texttt{Sepal.Length},
\texttt{Sepal.Width} will increase by 0.7985 units.

To judge whether they are statistically significant, examine the
p-values under the column
\texttt{Pr(\textgreater{}\textbar{}t\textbar{})}. The p-value of the
slope is close to zero, suggesting that it is statistically significant;
unfortunately, the intercept is not. It means that the linear
correlation between \texttt{Sepal.Width} and \texttt{Sepal.Length} is
strong. However, it is completely accurate to predict
\texttt{Sepal.Width} using \texttt{Sepal.Length}.

Since we mentioned correlation, the \texttt{Multiple\ R-squared} is the
square of Pearson's correlation between the two variables. Let's verify
this.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Pearson\textquotesingle{}s correlation}
\NormalTok{r }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{with}\NormalTok{(setosa\_iris, }\FunctionTok{cor}\NormalTok{(Sepal.Length, Sepal.Width))}

\FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{paste}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Pearson\textquotesingle{}s correlation ="}\NormalTok{,r))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "Pearson's correlation = 0.74254668566516"
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Square of Pearson\textquotesingle{}s correlation is Multiple R{-}squared}
\FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{paste}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"The square of r ="}\NormalTok{,r}\SpecialCharTok{**}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "The square of r = 0.551375580392314"
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Multiple Linear Regression}\label{sec-04-MLR}

A multiple linear regression (MLR) model involves one response and two
or more predictors. The equation for MLR is

\[Y = \beta_{0} + \beta_{1} \times X_{1} + ... + \beta_{n} \times X_{n}\]

Suppose we are keen to predict \texttt{Sepal.Width} by
\texttt{Sepal.Length} and \texttt{Petal.Length} for setosa iris. Similar
to SLR, we visualize the data but using a multipanel scatter plot
Section~\ref{sec-baseR-multipanel-scatter-plot},
Section~\ref{sec-ggplot-multipanel-scatter-plot}).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{pairs}\NormalTok{(setosa\_iris[,}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{], }\AttributeTok{main=}\StringTok{"A Multi{-}Panel Scatter Plot"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{04_Statistical_Tests_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-21-1.pdf}}

Here we visualize the trend between \texttt{Sepal.Width} and
\texttt{Sepal.Length} (2\textsuperscript{nd} row and
1\textsuperscript{st} column), \texttt{Sepal.Width} and
\texttt{Petal.Length} (2\textsuperscript{nd} row and
3\textsuperscript{rd} column), and \texttt{Sepal.Width} and
\texttt{Petal.Width} (2\textsuperscript{nd} row and
4\textsuperscript{th} column). Despite the trend not being clear between
\texttt{Sepal.Width} and \texttt{Petal.Length}, and \texttt{Sepal.Width}
and \texttt{Petal.Width}, let's proceed to build an MLR model.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{mlr\_model }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{lm}\NormalTok{(Sepal.Width }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ Sepal.Length }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ Petal.Length }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ Petal.Width, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{setosa\_iris)}
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(mlr\_model)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}

Call:
lm(formula = Sepal.Width ~ Sepal.Length + Petal.Length + Petal.Width, 
    data = setosa_iris)

Residuals:
     Min       1Q   Median       3Q      Max 
-0.74373 -0.17838  0.00164  0.16876  0.53988 

Coefficients:
             Estimate Std. Error t value Pr(>|t|)    
(Intercept)  -0.48913    0.57348  -0.853    0.398    
Sepal.Length  0.79669    0.11247   7.083 6.83e-09 ***
Petal.Length -0.07136    0.23244  -0.307    0.760    
Petal.Width   0.13513    0.38427   0.352    0.727    
---
Signif. codes:  0 '***' 0.001 '**' 0.01 '*' 0.05 '.' 0.1 ' ' 1

Residual standard error: 0.2616 on 46 degrees of freedom
Multiple R-squared:  0.553, Adjusted R-squared:  0.5239 
F-statistic: 18.97 on 3 and 46 DF,  p-value: 3.759e-08
\end{verbatim}

Based on the p-values, only \texttt{Sepal.Length} is a stable predictor
of \texttt{Sepal.Width}; \texttt{Petal.Length} and \texttt{Petal.Width}
do not make a statistically significant contribution to the model. This
example is simple, as there are not many predictors to be considered.
When the number of predictors rises, the number of choices explodes
exponentially. For a four-predictor example, the number of combinations
is:

\[\sum_{i=1}^{n=4} \binom{n}{i}\ = \binom{4}{1}\ + \binom{4}{2}\ + \binom{4}{3}\ + \binom{4}{4}\ = 1 + 6 + 6 + 1 = 14\]
R has a function to assist in finding a ``not too bad'' MLR model,
meaning it does not guarantee to return the best model. Here is how it
works. It begins with a one-predictor model; start with any predictor.
Then it tries to build an intermediate model by incorporating an
additional predictor from the pool that returns the least error. This
step will continue until all predictors are chosen.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{baseline\_mlr }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{lm}\NormalTok{(Sepal.Width }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ Sepal.Length, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{setosa\_iris)}
\FunctionTok{add1}\NormalTok{(baseline\_mlr, }\AttributeTok{scope =} \SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ Sepal.Length }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ Petal.Length }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ Petal.Width, }\AttributeTok{test =} \StringTok{"F"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Single term additions

Model:
Sepal.Width ~ Sepal.Length
             Df Sum of Sq    RSS     AIC F value Pr(>F)
<none>                    3.1587 -134.09               
Petal.Length  1 0.0032458 3.1554 -132.15  0.0483 0.8269
Petal.Width   1 0.0052586 3.1534 -132.18  0.0784 0.7807
\end{verbatim}

The model error is reflected in the Akaike Information Criterion (AIC)
values. The intuition is that more predictors will naturally fit the
data better. However, the model inevitably fits the noise as well. As a
result, although the model looks great in development, it eventually
fails to handle unseen data, i.e., overfitting. AIC serves as a canary.
As a rule of thumb, if the difference in AIC between consecutive
intermediate models is less than 2, there is no significant difference.

\section{Summary}\label{summary-2}

In this chapter, we have discussed classical statistical tests in the
framework of Null Hypothesis Significance Test (NHST). A simple
guideline for choosing the most appropriate test is to identify the main
variable(s), how many there are, and their type(s). The table below
summarizes the tests discussed in this chapter:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Name
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Description
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Example
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Reference
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
One-sample t-test & Test whether the mean of a quantitative variable
equals a specified value. &
\texttt{t.test(FlightDelays\$Delay,\ mu\ =\ 12)} &
Section~\ref{sec-04-one-sample-t-test} \\
Two-sample t-test & Test whether or not the means of two groups are
equal. &
\texttt{t.test(Delay\ \textasciitilde{}\ Carrier,\ data=FlightDelays)} &
Section~\ref{sec-04-two-sample-t-test} \\
One-way ANOVA & Test whether or not the means of three or more groups
are equal. &
\texttt{aov\_results\ \textless{}-\ aov(Delay\ \textasciitilde{}\ Destination,\ data=FlightDelays)}
& Section~\ref{sec-04-one-way-ANOVA} \\
Paired t-test & Test whether the mean difference between two
measurements taken on the same subject significantly differs from zero &
\texttt{with(Groceries,\ t.test(Target,\ Walmart,\ paired=T))} &
Section~\ref{sec-04-paired-t-test} \\
\(\chi^2\) goodness-of-fit & Test whether the proportions of categories
match a specified expected ratio. &
\texttt{chisq.test(table(FlightDelays\$Delayed30),\ p=c(0.9,0.1))} &
Section~\ref{sec-04-x2-gof-test} \\
\(\chi^2\) test for independence & Test the independence between two
categorical variables &
\texttt{with(FlightDelays,\ chisq.test(Carrier,\ Delayed30,\ correct=TRUE))}
& Section~\ref{sec-04-x2-test-independence} \\
Simple Linear Regression & It examines the linear relationship between
the predictor and the response. &
\texttt{lm(Sepal.Width\ \textasciitilde{}\ Sepal.Length,\ data=setosa\_iris)}
& Section~\ref{sec-04-SLR} \\
Multiple Linear Regression & It examines the linear relationship between
multiple predictors and the response. &
\texttt{lm(Sepal.Width\ \textasciitilde{}\ Sepal.Length\ +\ Petal.Length,\ data=setosa\_iris)}
& Section~\ref{sec-04-MLR} \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Exercises}\label{exercises}

Use the built-in data set \texttt{mtcars} to answer these questions.
Before tackling the questions below, you need to convert the categorical
variables in \texttt{mtcars} to a factor. Here is the code:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{mtcars}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cyl }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{factor}\NormalTok{(mtcars}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cyl)}
\NormalTok{mtcars}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{vs }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{factor}\NormalTok{(mtcars}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{vs, }\AttributeTok{levels =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{mtcars}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{am }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{factor}\NormalTok{(mtcars}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{am, }\AttributeTok{levels =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Q1. Test whether the mean \texttt{mpg} is 20 at the 95\% confidence
level.

Q2. \texttt{am} stands for automatic transmission. A 0 means not
automatic and 1 means automatic. Test whether the mean acceleration
\texttt{qsec} is the same between automatic and manual transmissions.

Q3. Test whether the mean acceleration \texttt{qsec} is the same for
different numbers of cylinders \texttt{cyl}. If it is not true, identify
the pair(s) that have different mean acceleration. Note that
\texttt{cyl} must be converted to a factor before performing any tests.

Q4. Test whether cars with different numbers of cylinders are equally
sampled in the data set.

Q5. Test whether the type of transmission \texttt{am} and the number of
cylinders are independent. Note: If you encounter a warning message, it
is due to small-count data. You can ignore it.

Q6. Use a linear model to test the relationship between horsepower
\texttt{hp} and acceleration \texttt{qsec}.

Q7. Use a multiple linear model to study the relationship between
acceleration, horsepower, weight, and MPG.

\chapter{Dive Deeper}\label{dive-deeper}

So far, we have discussed the basics of R that are sufficient for most
situations. In this chapter, we will discuss several practical
operations that require a more detailed discussion. That includes how to
create summary tables from published work, create a small data set in a
data frame, load a data set of any size into RStudio, define your own
function, and leverage the family of \texttt{apply()} functions.

\section*{Learning Objectives:}\label{learning-objectives-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Learning Objectives:}

\markright{Learning Objectives:}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Recreate a summary table from published work.
\item
  Create a small data frame with data.
\item
  Load a data set into RStudio.
\item
  Define your own functions.
\item
  Harness the family of \texttt{apply()} functions.
\end{itemize}

\section{Recreate a Summary Table in
R}\label{recreate-a-summary-table-in-r}

You see the data summary tables as those in @fig-5-1, so you want to
recreate and analyze them yourself. The \texttt{table()} or
\texttt{group\_by()} functions mentioned in the previous chapters cannot
help in this situation, as they require the raw data, like
\texttt{iris}, where the information of each sample is accessible.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=0.6\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{images/Figure_5-1-SummaryTable.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-5-1}Recreate summary tables}

\end{figure}%

Here you will create the two tables in R as shown in
Figure~\ref{fig-5-1}. Let us begin with the first table, which is a
contingency table with two rows and five columns. As all the values in
the table are numeric, each row is represented by a numeric vector.
Here, we will use the \texttt{c()} function to create it. See
Section~\ref{sec-01-vectors}. But the vector designated for the first
row here is slightly different because we want to specify the column
names and the values together (a \textbf{named vector}); as a result,
the columns in the resultant table will be named.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{row1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{heart\_disease=}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{formerly=}\DecValTok{808}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{never=}\DecValTok{1802}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{smoke=}\DecValTok{728}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{unknown=}\DecValTok{1496}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{row2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{77}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{90}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{61}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{48}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Note that the order of the values in the named vector \texttt{row1}
determines the order of the columns in the table. Ensure that the same
order is maintained for subsequent rows.

When all the rows have been defined, they will be stacked up into a
table using the \texttt{rbind()} function as shown below.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{my\_tab1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rbind}\NormalTok{(row1, row2)}
\NormalTok{my\_tab1}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
     heart_disease formerly never smoke unknown
row1             0      808  1802   728    1496
row2             1       77    90    61      48
\end{verbatim}

Note that names \texttt{row1} and \texttt{row2} on the left are the
names of the rows (\texttt{rownames}), they do not constitute a column
of the table. Hence, it is a 2x5 table as shown by the dimension
function \texttt{dim()} below.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{dim}\NormalTok{(my\_tab1)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 2 5
\end{verbatim}

Now, you can plot the table Section~\ref{sec-03-contingency-table}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{barplot}\NormalTok{(my\_tab1, }\AttributeTok{main=}\StringTok{"Recreate a Table"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{beside=}\NormalTok{T,}
        \AttributeTok{legend.text =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"No heart disease"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"heart disease"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{05_Dive_Deeper_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-5-1.pdf}}

Or, perform a \(\chi^{2}\) test of independence.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{chisq.test}\NormalTok{(my\_tab1, }\AttributeTok{correct=}\NormalTok{F)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Warning in chisq.test(my_tab1, correct = F): Chi-squared approximation may be
incorrect
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

    Pearson's Chi-squared test

data:  my_tab1
X-squared = 61.967, df = 4, p-value = 1.119e-12
\end{verbatim}

The second table in Figure~\ref{fig-5-1} is different from the first one
as each row contains a mixture of character
(\texttt{daily\ tobacco\ onsumption} ) and numeric values
(\texttt{controls} and \texttt{cases}), making vectors unsuitable for
this situation. R provides an object similar to vectors, ordered
sequences, that is the \texttt{list} object. The main difference between
a vector and a list is that the latter allows values to have different
data types, i.e., \textbf{heterogeneous}. Below is an example to define
rows as list objects:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{A }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{group=}\StringTok{"A"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{daily\_toba=}\StringTok{"0{-}9"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ncontrols=}\DecValTok{90}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ncases=}\DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{B }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"B"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"10{-}19"}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{44}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{C }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"20{-}29"}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{25}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{D }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"D"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"30+"}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{11}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Similarly, the row (\texttt{A}) defines the values and column names of
the resultant table. Now, rows are stacked up together to form a 4x4
table.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{my\_tab2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rbind}\NormalTok{(A,B,C,D)}
\NormalTok{my\_tab2}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
  group daily_toba ncontrols ncases
A "A"   "0-9"      90        14    
B "B"   "10-19"    44        13    
C "C"   "20-29"    25        8     
D "D"   "30+"      8         11    
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{dim}\NormalTok{(my\_tab2))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 4 4
\end{verbatim}

\section{Create a small data frame with data in
R}\label{create-a-small-data-frame-with-data-in-r}

Here we will discuss two approaches to store a small data set in a
\texttt{data.frame} object. If the data set is large, my recommendation
is to store the data in a spreadsheet, e.g., Google Sheets or Microsoft
Excel, and then upload it into RStudio. See the next section for more
details.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Use \texttt{data.frame()}
Function}{Use data.frame() Function}}\label{use-data.frame-function}

Here is the first approach to create a data frame that takes two steps.
The first step is to define each column (variable) as a vector, and then
use the \texttt{data.frame()} function to combine them into a data
frame. Here is an example to create a data frame with two columns:
\texttt{group}, and systolic blood pressure (\texttt{sbp}), and eight
rows. Two vectors, each with eight values, are created below:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{group\_data }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"A"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"A"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"A"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"A"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"A"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"B"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"B"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"B"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{sbp\_data }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{120}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{135}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{133}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{128}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{130}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{150}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{160}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{158}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Next, use the \texttt{data.frame()} function to combine the vectors into
a data frame.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{small\_df1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{group =}\NormalTok{ group\_data,}
                       \AttributeTok{sbp =}\NormalTok{ sbp\_data)}
\NormalTok{small\_df1}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
  group sbp
1     A 120
2     A 135
3     A 133
4     A 128
5     A 130
6     B 150
7     B 160
8     B 158
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Use \texttt{read.table()}
Function}{Use read.table() Function}}\label{use-read.table-function}

The second approach is to type out the values in columns as in the
example below, and feed it to the \texttt{read.table()} function through
the \texttt{text=} parameter.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{content }\OtherTok{=}\NormalTok{ (}\StringTok{"}
\StringTok{group sbp}
\StringTok{A 120}
\StringTok{A 135}
\StringTok{A 133}
\StringTok{A 128}
\StringTok{A 130}
\StringTok{B 150}
\StringTok{B 160}
\StringTok{B 158}
\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{small\_df2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read.table}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{text =}\NormalTok{ content, }\AttributeTok{header =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{small\_df2}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
  group sbp
1     A 120
2     A 135
3     A 133
4     A 128
5     A 130
6     B 150
7     B 160
8     B 158
\end{verbatim}

There is no good or bad of either approach unless you want to use some R
functions to generate the column vectors. In such a case, the first
approach using \texttt{data.frame()} function is more trackable.

\section{Load Data Sets into RStudio}\label{load-data-sets-into-rstudio}

Data sets are usually too large to be typed using the methods discussed
in the previous section. Here, you will learn several ways to load a
data set stored in a file into RStudio. The two common data file formats
are: comma-separated (\texttt{.csv}) or tab-separated (\texttt{.txt} or
\texttt{.tsv}). In a \texttt{.csv} file, the comma (\texttt{,}) serves
as a column separator, separating data in one column from the other
column, as shown in panel A of Figure~\ref{fig-5-2}. The other common
separator is the \texttt{tab} value, which is usually invisible but is
deliberately made visible in panel B.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{images/Figure_5-2-FileFormats.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-5-2}File formats}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{read.csv()} and
\texttt{read.table()}
Functions}{read.csv() and read.table() Functions}}\label{read.csv-and-read.table-functions}

R provides two functions to read the data file directly into RStudio.
\texttt{read.csv()} reads a \texttt{.csv} file from the location
provided. The location can be a URL or a file path to a file on your
computer. For example, load the flight delays file from here:
\url{https://www.openintro.org/data/csv/airline_delay.csv}. The first
parameter is the file location. Make sure that it is in quotes.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{delays1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read.csv}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"https://www.openintro.org/data/csv/airline\_delay.csv"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{stringsAsFactors =}\NormalTok{ T)}
\FunctionTok{head}\NormalTok{(delays1)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
  year month carrier      carrier_name airport
1 2020    12      9E Endeavor Air Inc.     ABE
2 2020    12      9E Endeavor Air Inc.     ABY
3 2020    12      9E Endeavor Air Inc.     AEX
4 2020    12      9E Endeavor Air Inc.     AGS
5 2020    12      9E Endeavor Air Inc.     ALB
6 2020    12      9E Endeavor Air Inc.     ATL
                                                 airport_name arr_flights
1 Allentown/Bethlehem/Easton, PA: Lehigh Valley International          44
2                      Albany, GA: Southwest Georgia Regional          90
3                    Alexandria, LA: Alexandria International          88
4                 Augusta, GA: Augusta Regional at Bush Field         184
5                            Albany, NY: Albany International          76
6       Atlanta, GA: Hartsfield-Jackson Atlanta International        5985
  arr_del15 carrier_ct weather_ct nas_ct security_ct late_aircraft_ct
1         3       1.63       0.00   0.12           0             1.25
2         1       0.96       0.00   0.04           0             0.00
3         8       5.75       0.00   1.60           0             0.65
4         9       4.17       0.00   1.83           0             3.00
5        11       4.78       0.00   5.22           0             1.00
6       445     142.89      11.96 161.37           1           127.79
  arr_cancelled arr_diverted arr_delay carrier_delay weather_delay nas_delay
1             0            1        89            56             0         3
2             0            0        23            22             0         1
3             0            1       338           265             0        45
4             0            0       508           192             0        92
5             1            0       692           398             0       178
6             5            0     30756         16390          1509      5060
  security_delay late_aircraft_delay
1              0                  30
2              0                   0
3              0                  28
4              0                 224
5              0                 116
6             16                7781
\end{verbatim}

The \texttt{stringAsFactors=T} tells the function to treat columns with
text values as a categorical variable (factor), which is common in
statistical analysis.

If the separator is a \texttt{tab} or something else,
\texttt{read.table()} allows us to specify the actual separator. For
example, another version of the same flight delays file is in
tab-separated format. So, \texttt{tab} is used as the column separator
of flight delays.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{delays2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read.table}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"https://www.openintro.org/data/tab{-}delimited/airline\_delay.txt"}\NormalTok{,}
                      \AttributeTok{header =}\NormalTok{ T,}
                      \AttributeTok{sep =} \StringTok{"}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}t}\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{,}
                      \AttributeTok{stringsAsFactors =}\NormalTok{ T)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Warning in scan(file = file, what = what, sep = sep, quote = quote, dec = dec,
: EOF within quoted string
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}
Warning in scan(file = file, what = what, sep = sep, quote = quote, dec = dec,
: number of items read is not a multiple of the number of columns
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{head}\NormalTok{(delays2)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
  year month carrier      carrier_name airport
1 2020    12      9E Endeavor Air Inc.     ABE
2 2020    12      9E Endeavor Air Inc.     ABY
3 2020    12      9E Endeavor Air Inc.     AEX
4 2020    12      9E Endeavor Air Inc.     AGS
5 2020    12      9E Endeavor Air Inc.     ALB
6 2020    12      9E Endeavor Air Inc.     ATL
                                                 airport_name arr_flights
1 Allentown/Bethlehem/Easton, PA: Lehigh Valley International          44
2                      Albany, GA: Southwest Georgia Regional          90
3                    Alexandria, LA: Alexandria International          88
4                 Augusta, GA: Augusta Regional at Bush Field         184
5                            Albany, NY: Albany International          76
6       Atlanta, GA: Hartsfield-Jackson Atlanta International        5985
  arr_del15 carrier_ct weather_ct nas_ct security_ct late_aircraft_ct
1         3       1.63       0.00   0.12           0             1.25
2         1       0.96       0.00   0.04           0             0.00
3         8       5.75       0.00   1.60           0             0.65
4         9       4.17       0.00   1.83           0             3.00
5        11       4.78       0.00   5.22           0             1.00
6       445     142.89      11.96 161.37           1           127.79
  arr_cancelled arr_diverted arr_delay carrier_delay weather_delay nas_delay

6             16                7781
\end{verbatim}

The \texttt{tab} on the keyboard is denoted by
\texttt{\textbackslash{}t}, no space between the two characters.
Additionally, not all data files contain a column heading in the first
row. By default, R does not assume one exists. To override the default,
like the above example, specify \texttt{header=T}.

\subsection{Import data in RStudio}\label{import-data-in-rstudio}

Suppose the data file has been downloaded on your computer in the
\texttt{Downloads} subfolder. The first step is to upload it to the
RStudio Cloud Drive via \textbf{Files-\textgreater Upload} inside the
Files panel, usually on the bottom right.

Next, import the data file into RStudio via \textbf{Environment
-\textgreater{} Import Dataset -\textgreater{} From Text (base)} as
shown below:

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=0.6\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{images/Figure_5-3-Environ-Import.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-5-3}Import Data Set}

\end{figure}%

Select the file to be imported as shown in Figure 5-4 below:

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=0.6\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{images/Figure_5-4-select-file.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-5-4}Select file}

\end{figure}%

A window will pop up as shown in Figure 5-4, which is a preview of the
data. Do the following:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Check \texttt{heading\ is\ Yes} box.
\item
  Check \texttt{StringAsFactors} box.
\item
  Click the \texttt{Import} button at the bottom.
\end{itemize}

This imports the data file into RStudio and reflects it in the
\textbf{Environment} panel on the top right.

\section{Define Functions}\label{define-functions}

When no built-in functions do what you want, it is the occasion to write
your own functions. A function consists of input, process, and output. A
daily analogy is the vending machine. The inputs are money and choice;
the process is the mechanical system to fetch the selected beverage,
snack, etc., and the output is the dispensed item.

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{images/Illustrate_5-1.png}}

}

\caption{Illustration 5.1 Function.}

\end{figure}%

Here are the four components required to define a function: a function
name, argument(s), the body, and the output(s).

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Function name: same as the rules in defining variables.
\item
  Argument(s): A function may have zero, 1, 2+ arguments or inputs.
\item
  Body: R code that takes the input argument(s), if any, performs the
  task, and returns the output.
\item
  Output(s): the value(s), object(s), or none returned to the caller.
\end{itemize}

Suppose we want to convert temperature from Fahrenheit to Celsius,
\(C=\frac{5}{9} \times (F-32)\), with specified precision; the default
is zero. Here is the function definition:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{F2C }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(f, }\AttributeTok{decimals =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{) \{}
  \FunctionTok{return}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{((f}\DecValTok{{-}32}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{*}\DecValTok{5}\SpecialCharTok{/}\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{, decimals))}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The function name is \texttt{F2C}. The function takes two arguments:
\texttt{f} that denotes the temperature in Fahrenheit, and the number of
decimals, with the default zero. Note that arguments are not required to
have default values. The body consists of one line of code:
\texttt{return(round((f-32)*5/9,\ decimals))}. The output is the result
of the arithmetic expression.

Let's test it out.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{temp1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{F2C}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{paste}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"100 F is equivalent to"}\NormalTok{,temp1, }\StringTok{"celsius"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "100 F is equivalent to 38 celsius"
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{temp2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{F2C}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{decimals =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{paste}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"{-}10 F is equivalent to"}\NormalTok{,temp2, }\StringTok{"celsius"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] "-10 F is equivalent to -23.33 celsius"
\end{verbatim}

\section{The Apply-Like Family}\label{the-apply-like-family}

The \texttt{apply()} family has seven members. Here we will focus on
three members that will do 80\% of the job: \texttt{apply()},
\texttt{sapply()}, and \texttt{lappy()}.

\subsection{Mechanic of Apply-Like
Functions}\label{mechanic-of-apply-like-functions}

Generally, apply-like functions execute a function over every value of
an R composite object
Section~\ref{sec-01-atomic-and-composite-variables}. While
\texttt{apply()} evaluates along the rows or columns of 2D objects like
matrices and data frames, functions like \texttt{lapply()} or
\texttt{sapply()} iterate over elements in 1D objects like vectors and
lists. The function that acts on each value of the targeted object can
be an R built-in function or a user-defined function. More details about
the apply-like functions will be discussed below.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{lappy()}}{lappy()}}\label{lappy}

It executes a function over every value of a \texttt{list} or a
\texttt{vector}, not 2D objects, and returns a \texttt{list}. For
example, change the 50 state names specified in the built-in list
\texttt{state.name} to uppercase.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{state.name.upper }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{lapply}\NormalTok{(state.name, }\AttributeTok{FUN=}\NormalTok{toupper)}
\FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(state.name.upper[}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{])}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[[1]]
[1] "ALABAMA"

[[2]]
[1] "ALASKA"

[[3]]
[1] "ARIZONA"

[[4]]
[1] "ARKANSAS"

[[5]]
[1] "CALIFORNIA"
\end{verbatim}

You may notice the output looks strange, despite the conversion being
correct. Please hold on to the question until the next section.

A note about the syntax. \texttt{toupper()} is a built-in function that
changes the input text to uppercase. When we provide the function to any
apply-like functions, \textbf{specify the function's name; do not
include the parenthesis \texttt{(\ )}.}

Let's look at another example of how to pass an argument to the
function. The built-in data \texttt{precip} recorded the average amount
of annual precipitation (in inches) for 70 US cities. Suppose we want to
convert inches to centimeters (cm) with precision provided by the
caller; the default is zero.

Let's define a customized function for the conversion.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{inch2cm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(inches, }\AttributeTok{decimals =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{  cm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(inches }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FloatTok{2.54}\NormalTok{, decimals)}
  \FunctionTok{return}\NormalTok{(cm)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Next, convert all values in \texttt{precip} to cm using
\texttt{lapply()}. In this call, the conversion is rounded up to the
nearest whole number.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{precip\_cm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{lapply}\NormalTok{(precip, }\AttributeTok{FUN=}\NormalTok{inch2cm)}
\FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(precip\_cm[}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{])}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
$Mobile
[1] 170

$Juneau
[1] 139

$Phoenix
[1] 18
\end{verbatim}

Pass an additional argument, decimals=2, to \texttt{inch2cm} in the
\texttt{lapply()} function.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{precip\_cm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{lapply}\NormalTok{(precip, }\AttributeTok{FUN=}\NormalTok{inch2cm, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(precip\_cm[}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{])}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
$Mobile
[1] 170.18

$Juneau
[1] 138.94

$Phoenix
[1] 17.78
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{sapply()}}{sapply()}}\label{sapply}

The ``s'' of \texttt{sapply()} stands for simplified. It functions
almost exactly like \texttt{lapply()} except that it returns a vector or
matrix. Recall that \texttt{lappy()} always returns a \texttt{list}
regardless of the input.

Here's the same process to convert precipitation from inches to cm but
using \texttt{sapply()}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{precip\_cm\_vector }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sapply}\NormalTok{(precip, }\AttributeTok{FUN =}\NormalTok{ inch2cm, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(precip\_cm\_vector[}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{])}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
 Mobile  Juneau Phoenix 
 170.18  138.94   17.78 
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{apply()}}{apply()}}\label{apply}

Here we will expand the apply operation on 2D objects like matrices or
data frames using \texttt{apply()}. You can direct the function to all
values in a row, a column, or both. The \texttt{apply()} function takes
three arguments: a 1D or 2D object, margin, and function. Margin 1 means
to apply the function row by row; 2 means column by column. If both are
given, the function will process all values of the 2D object.

\begin{figure}[H]

{\centering \pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{images/Illustrate_5-2.png}}

}

\caption{Illustration 5.1 Margins of apply().}

\end{figure}%

For example, calculate the column mean of the four numeric variables in
\texttt{iris}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{apply}\NormalTok{(iris[,}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{], }\AttributeTok{MARGIN =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{FUN=}\NormalTok{mean)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Sepal.Length  Sepal.Width Petal.Length  Petal.Width 
    5.843333     3.057333     3.758000     1.199333 
\end{verbatim}

Convert all values from inches to cm in \texttt{iris}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{iris\_cm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{apply}\NormalTok{(iris[,}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{], }\AttributeTok{MARGIN =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{FUN =}\NormalTok{ inch2cm)}
\FunctionTok{head}\NormalTok{(iris\_cm)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
     Sepal.Length Sepal.Width Petal.Length Petal.Width
[1,]           13           9            4           1
[2,]           12           8            4           1
[3,]           12           8            3           1
[4,]           12           8            4           1
[5,]           13           9            4           1
[6,]           14          10            4           1
\end{verbatim}
